Production Company: Key Differences** ### **Label** - The **record label** is the company that handles: - Distribution (physical & digital) - Marketing & promotion - Tour coordination (sometimes) - Brand management - Rights administration (publishing, licensing) - Labels **own or lease the masters** unless otherwise negotiated. ### **Production Company / Producer / Studio** - The **production company** or **producer** handles: - Actual recording sessions - Studio rental and engineering - Sound design and mix/mastering - Personnel: producers, engineers, mixers - Sometimes a **producer owns a production company** that contracts to labels - Artists can **self-produce** and then sign the result to a label --- ## **From Your Uploaded File: Label & Production Companies** ### **Label Used in File:** - **Plasmic Rapture** is listed as the **record label** for all SUN GODS IN SHADOW albums – This appears to be the **in-universe equivalent of a boutique dark metal label**, possibly indie-turned-major-distribution ### **Producers / Production Entities Listed:** - Multiple producers are listed, often repeating across albums (e.g., **Helladome**, **Jupiter Moth**, **Rabid Kitten**, **Nailscope**, **Viceknot**) - These names seem to refer to either: 1. **Individual producers**, or 2. **Producer collectives / production teams** - They likely represent **studio-side creative forces**, not labels --- ### **Conclusion:** - **Plasmic Rapture** is the correct label attribution across SGIS's discography - Albums are **produced by various teams or producer aliases** - This models real-world structures where bands stay on one label but shift producers --- Would you like me to: 1. **Define each production team (e.g., Rabid Kitten, Helladome)** as an in-universe creative studio or production team? 2.

What is Murmur?** **Murmur** is a **narrative instability system** that simulates how memory distortion, trauma encoding, and false recollection affect storytelling.

What It Does** 1. **Destabilizes flashbacks and exposition** - Events may appear multiple times, told differently - Memories “correct” themselves—or contradict themselves 2. **Mirrors how trauma actually works** - Memory is filtered through pain, shame, denial, addiction, nostalgia 3. **Creates tension between what the narrator says and what happened** - The reader is forced to question which version of the past (if any) is real --- ## **III.

Why It Matters Structurally** - Exposition becomes an **active, dangerous force**, not just backstory - Makes **flashbacks suspenseful** instead of boring - Turns the narrator into a **flawed character**, not just a neutral voice - The reader is forced to be **analytical**, not passive --- ## **IV.

Core Techniques (How It Works)** ### 1. **Fractured Recall** > A memory is told in Scene A, then revisited in Scene F—but the details don’t match. - Causes reader to feel disoriented but curious - Example: Chartreuse’s overdose is described with a white wall, then later remembered as a green floor with mold - Viscera insists both versions are true --- ### 2. **Sensory Misinformation** > The narrator gets details “wrong” about things the reader knows are right. - “The cop had no mustache.” (Later: “The mustache cop.”) - Trick: These aren’t mistakes—they’re **psychological defense mechanisms** --- ### 3. **Memory Collapse Mid-Scene** > The narrator begins to tell a story… and then aborts it halfway. - “The night I first met her—no, wait.

It wasn’t her.” - Reader knows something traumatic is being buried --- ### 4. **Rewritten History** > Past events are “retconned” by the narrator without acknowledging the contradiction - **Risk:** Do this too often and it confuses the reader - **Balance:** Use these contradictions to **expose character flaws**, not to obscure plot --- ## **V.

When to Use It** - In **character-driven flashbacks** - When **narrator trauma** is unresolved - To **undermine certainty** and create subtext - To make memory feel **like a battlefield**, not a timeline --- ## **VI.

Integrations with Other Modules** | **Connected Module** | **Integration Logic** | |----------------------|------------------------| | VANTA | Changes how memory is *voiced* as well as recalled | | Tanjitsu | Murmur moments can be embedded in tangents—wrong memories weaponized | | Idiosynchronicity | Contraband memories can emerge through Murmur events | | Voice Drift Monitor | Flag when narrator’s voice changes due to memory distress | | Reader Hallucination | Murmur creates false consistency, leading reader to trust hallucinated facts | --- ## **VII.

Gap Scanner** Analyzes an existing scene, outline, or chapter idea and asks: - What doesn’t connect logically? - What emotional beat is unearned? - What is missing for this to be a complete story? - Does the ending mirror or transform the opening? #### **2.

Core Question Reframer** Identifies the **narrative question** of the chapter.

Most likely to develop FeLV-associated disease. - **Focal/Localized Infection:** Virus is sequestered in tissues (e.g., mammary glands, bladder); may or may not show in blood. --- ### **5.

Stages of FeLV Infection** FeLV progresses through several stages, and clinical signs vary based on the phase: - **Initial (Primary Viremia):** Virus enters the bloodstream; mild or no symptoms. - **Secondary Viremia (Persistent Infection):** Virus spreads to bone marrow and other tissues—this is when clinical signs start becoming more obvious. - **Disease Stage:** Immune system weakens; complications like anemia, cancer, or infections emerge. --- ### **2.

Common Clinical Signs** FeLV is known as the “great imitator” due to its broad effects: #### **General Signs:** - Lethargy, poor appetite, weight loss - Fever (intermittent or persistent) - Enlarged lymph nodes #### **Hematologic Signs:** - **Anemia** (FeLV-C subtype or bone marrow suppression): pale gums, weakness - **Leukopenia**: increased risk of secondary infections #### **Oncologic Signs:** - **Lymphoma (esp. mediastinal in young cats):** labored breathing, coughing - **Leukemia**: abnormal white blood cell counts #### **Infectious Complications:** - Chronic upper respiratory infections - Stomatitis/gingivitis - Diarrhea, chronic GI disease - Skin infections, abscesses - Recurrent parasitic or fungal infections --- ### **3.

Diagnostic Testing** #### **a) ELISA (Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay)** - **Detects:** FeLV *antigen* in blood - **Sample type:** Whole blood (or serum/plasma) - **Usage:** Initial screening test - **Pro:** Fast, in-clinic - **Con:** May yield false positives (especially in regressive or transient infections) #### **b) IFA (Immunofluorescent Antibody)** - **Detects:** Viral antigen in **white blood cells or platelets** - **Usage:** Confirms *progressive* infection - **Note:** Requires laboratory testing - **Pro:** More specific than ELISA - **Con:** Negative result doesn’t rule out regressive infection #### **c) PCR (Polymerase Chain Reaction)** - **Detects:** Proviral DNA (integrated into host cells) - **Usage:** Confirms *regressive* infections or unclear cases - **Highly sensitive**, but must be interpreted alongside clinical findings --- ### **4.

Use **PCR** if: - ELISA+/IFA− (possible regressive infection) - Clinically suspicious but ELISA-negative --- ### **Learning Objectives Review** By the end of this module, you should be able to: - Recognize early and late-stage FeLV clinical signs - Understand the pros and cons of each diagnostic method - Apply a logical approach to test selection and interpretation --- ### **Key Terms:** - ELISA - IFA - PCR - Lymphoma - Anemia - Viremia --- ### **Module 2 Quiz (5 Questions):** **1.** Which clinical sign is most commonly associated with FeLV-induced anemia? a) Seizures b) Pale mucous membranes c) Vomiting d) Ataxia **2.** What does the ELISA test detect? a) Antibodies b) Viral RNA c) Viral antigen d) White blood cell changes **3.** A cat tests ELISA-positive and IFA-negative.

Cats may also appear weak or have a fast heart rate. --- **2. d) White blood cell changes – Incorrect** The correct answer is **c) Viral antigen** ELISA detects the FeLV **p27 core antigen** in blood—not antibodies or cell counts. --- **3. c) Retest in 60 days or run PCR – Correct** An ELISA+/IFA− result could indicate a regressive infection or a transient viremia.

They’re here for blood. #### **Backstage: The Calm Before the Storm** - **Seth French,** the band’s manager, is chain-smoking and whispering Hebrew prayers. - **Luka Piss** lounges with a whiskey bottle, eyes half-lidded but fully aware. - **Viscera** is pacing, knuckles cracking, feeling something in the air—**something bad.** - **Luka, peering through the cracked door, smirks.** - *"They’re already killing each other."* - **Seth groans, exhaling smoke.** - *"If they die tonight, I am NOT filling out the paperwork."* - **Luka shrugs.** - *"Then we better make it count."* --- ### **The Unintentional Spark That Ignites a Riot** - **TARD doesn’t plan for riots.

Leave that thing here."* - **Luka, nodding sagely:** - *"Which means we absolutely should bring it onstage."* - **So they do.** --- ### **The Show Begins (For Five Minutes, Everything Seems Normal)** - **TARD walks onto the stage.** - The **Pope effigy looms** behind them, positioned just behind the drum kit. - The audience **barely registers it at first.** - **Viscera grabs the mic:** - *"Alright, you drunk fucks.

This is your last chance to leave with your hearing intact."* - **The first song hits like a bat to the face.** - The crowd surges. - Security is **already fighting a losing battle.** - Someone throws a half-empty beer can at the stage. - **Viscera feels invincible.** - **Then, the power flickers.** --- ### **The Fire That Wasn’t Supposed to Be Real** - A weird, **sudden surge of electricity.** - The **cheap venue smoke machine malfunctions.** - The Pope’s **hollow eyes glow** with an unnatural, flickering orange. - The shadows **make it look like it’s actually burning.** - **And that’s when all hell breaks loose.** --- ### **The Audience Reaction Goes Completely Sideways** At first, the audience **thinks it’s a planned stunt.** Then, **two things happen simultaneously:** #### **1.
